========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I guess work is why I’m here, mostly. I’m a waitress, and things have been getting weird enough that people noticed. Like during service, when it’s busy and I should be closing checks or running drinks, I’ll just sort of freeze. Not like I’m resting—more like I’m stuck.

The thing that scared everyone was I was standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and instead of doing what I was supposed to do, I just kept repeating table numbers. Like, table 12, table 7, table 12… and I couldn’t get myself back in the right order. I’d start doing steps wrong, or out of order, and then the checks wouldn’t get closed. People were staring, and my coworkers got worried because I wasn’t making sense in the middle of service.

So… yeah. That’s what brought me in. It’s embarrassing, but it’s been happening enough that it’s not something I can just explain away as being tired or distracted.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… I’m usually aware, I think. It doesn’t feel totally blank, like I disappeared or anything. I can remember pieces afterward, sometimes too clearly, actually—like standing by the soda station and realizing I had the cash drawer key in my hand, and people were waiting on me, and I was supposed to be closing checks.

But it’s like the normal order of things gets scrambled. I’ll know I’m at work, I’ll know there are tables and checks and drinks, but I can’t make the next step happen right. That time, I kept saying table numbers—table 12, table 7, table 12—and I knew those were table numbers, but I wasn’t doing what I was supposed to do with them. I wasn’t closing anything, I was just stuck there repeating them.

Afterward I remember coworkers looking at me and being worried, and I felt embarrassed because it wasn’t like I chose to stop working. It’s more like I’m there, but I can’t get myself back into the right sequence.

It’s usually not just a couple seconds. I mean, the freezing part can start suddenly, like I’ll be moving around doing my normal work and then I’m just… stopped. But it lasts long enough that people notice and start reacting to me, not just like I paused to think.

At the soda station thing, it felt like I was standing there for a while holding the cash drawer key, repeating the table numbers instead of closing the checks. I don’t know exactly how many minutes because I’m not looking at a clock, and when it’s happening time feels kind of weird, but it’s long enough that service gets backed up and my coworkers have to step in or say my name a few times. Then even after I start moving again, I’m not immediately normal-normal. I’m still trying to figure out what order I’m supposed to do things in.

It’s more like I’m there, but I can’t get myself back into the right sequence.\n\nDoctor: When one of these episodes happens, about how long does it usually last—seconds, a couple of minutes, or longer—before you're back to functioning normally?\n\nPatient: It’s usually not just a couple seconds. I mean, the freezing part can start suddenly, like I’ll be moving around doing my normal work and then I’m just… stopped. But it lasts long enough that people notice and start reacting to me, not just like I paused to think.\n\nAt the soda station thing, it felt like I was standing there for a while holding the cash drawer key, repeating the table numbers instead of closing the checks. I don’t know exactly how many minutes because I’m not looking at a clock, and when it’s happening time feels kind of weird, but it’s long enough that service gets backed up and my coworkers have to step in or say my name a few times. Then even after I start moving again, I’m not immediately normal-normal. I’m still trying to figure out what order I’m supposed to do things in.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these episodes, do you notice any involuntary movements—like lip-smacking, chewing motions, or fumbling hand movements—that happen without you meaning to do them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s like lip-smacking or chewing, not that anyone’s told me, anyway. What people noticed wasn’t really some small movement like that—it was more that I would just freeze in the middle of service, or start doing things completely out of order.

Like the soda station thing… I was just standing there holding the cash drawer key, and instead of closing checks like I was supposed to, I kept repeating table numbers. Table 12, table 7, table 12—like my brain grabbed the wrong piece of the job and got stuck on it. So my hands weren’t doing the next step, I wasn’t moving on to the register or the checks, I was just sort of stuck there with the key.

So… I guess if my hands are doing anything weird, I’m not aware of it. The weird part is more that I stop functioning right, or I’m doing the wrong step in the wrong order, and everyone around me can tell something’s off.

It’s more like I’m there, but I can’t get myself back into the right sequence.\n\nDoctor: When one of these episodes happens, about how long does it usually last—seconds, a couple of minutes, or longer—before you're back to functioning normally?\n\nPatient: It’s usually not just a couple seconds. I mean, the freezing part can start suddenly, like I’ll be moving around doing my normal work and then I’m just… stopped. But it lasts long enough that people notice and start reacting to me, not just like I paused to think.\n\nAt the soda station thing, it felt like I was standing there for a while holding the cash drawer key, repeating the table numbers instead of closing the checks. I don’t know exactly how many minutes because I’m not looking at a clock, and when it’s happening time feels kind of weird, but it’s long enough that service gets backed up and my coworkers have to step in or say my name a few times. Then even after I start moving again, I’m not immediately normal-normal. I’m still trying to figure out what order I’m supposed to do things in.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you notice any involuntary movements—like lip-smacking, chewing motions, or fumbling hand movements—that happen without you meaning to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like lip-smacking or chewing, not that anyone’s told me, anyway. What people noticed wasn’t really some small movement like that—it was more that I would just freeze in the middle of service, or start doing things completely out of order.\n\nLike the soda station thing… I was just standing there holding the cash drawer key, and instead of closing checks like I was supposed to, I kept repeating table numbers. Table 12, table 7, table 12—like my brain grabbed the wrong piece of the job and got stuck on it. So my hands weren’t doing the next step, I wasn’t moving on to the register or the checks, I was just sort of stuck there with the key.\n\nSo… I guess if my hands are doing anything weird, I’m not aware of it. The weird part is more that I stop functioning right, or I’m doing the wrong step in the wrong order, and everyone around me can tell something’s off.\n\n[Last Question]\nRight before one of these episodes starts, do you ever notice any warning signs—like a strange smell or taste, a feeling of déjà vu, or a rising sensation in your stomach—just before you freeze up?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing. I’ve tried to think about that, because afterward people ask me, like, “Did you feel it coming?” and I want there to be some clear signal, because then maybe I could catch it before it happens, you know?

But I don’t remember a strange smell or taste. I don’t get, like, a warning where my stomach rises or anything like that—at least not that I’ve noticed. And déjà vu… I mean, sometimes things feel off or overly intense when I’m stressed, but right before the freezing? I can’t say there’s a specific “oh, this is starting now” feeling.

It’s more like I’m doing the task, and then suddenly the task doesn’t connect to the next step anymore. Like I’ll be in the middle of work and then I realize I’m stuck, but by the time I realize that, it’s already happening. So if there is a warning sign, I’m not catching it.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if it’s stiff like… like someone could lift my arm and it would stay there. I don’t think anyone has tried to move my arms or legs during it, at least not that I remember.

What people at work noticed was more that I’d just sort of stop in the middle of service, like my body and brain weren’t going to the next step. The soda station thing is the one everyone keeps bringing up because I was standing there holding the cash drawer key, and I wasn’t closing the checks like I was supposed to. I kept repeating table numbers instead, like, “table 12, table 7,” over and over, and I couldn’t seem to switch back to what I was actually doing.

So I may be standing still in a weird way, because I’m frozen there, but I don’t know if it’s rigidity exactly. It’s more like I get stuck—standing there, holding something, not moving on—and everyone around me realizes something is wrong before I can really explain it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess work is why I’m here, mostly. I’m a waitress, and things have been getting weird enough that people noticed. Like during service, when it’s busy and I should be closing checks or running drinks, I’ll just sort of freeze. Not like I’m resting—more like I’m stuck.\n\nThe thing that scared everyone was I was standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key, and instead of doing what I was supposed to do, I just kept repeating table numbers. Like, table 12, table 7, table 12… and I couldn’t get myself back in the right order. I’d start doing steps wrong, or out of order, and then the checks wouldn’t get closed. People were staring, and my coworkers got worried because I wasn’t making sense in the middle of service.\n\nSo… yeah. That’s what brought me in. It’s embarrassing, but it’s been happening enough that it’s not something I can just explain away as being tired or distracted.\n\nDoctor: During one of these episodes, are you aware of what's happening around you and able to remember it afterward, or does it feel like a blank period you can't recall?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m usually aware, I think. It doesn’t feel totally blank, like I disappeared or anything. I can remember pieces afterward, sometimes too clearly, actually—like standing by the soda station and realizing I had the cash drawer key in my hand, and people were waiting on me, and I was supposed to be closing checks.\n\nBut it’s like the normal order of things gets scrambled. I’ll know I’m at work, I’ll know there are tables and checks and drinks, but I can’t make the next step happen right. That time, I kept saying table numbers—table 12, table 7, table 12—and I knew those were table numbers, but I wasn’t doing what I was supposed to do with them. I wasn’t closing anything, I was just stuck there repeating them.\n\nAfterward I remember coworkers looking at me and being worried, and I felt embarrassed because it wasn’t like I chose to stop working. It’s more like I’m there, but I can’t get myself back into the right sequence.\n\nDoctor: When one of these episodes happens, about how long does it usually last—seconds, a couple of minutes, or longer—before you're back to functioning normally?\n\nPatient: It’s usually not just a couple seconds. I mean, the freezing part can start suddenly, like I’ll be moving around doing my normal work and then I’m just… stopped. But it lasts long enough that people notice and start reacting to me, not just like I paused to think.\n\nAt the soda station thing, it felt like I was standing there for a while holding the cash drawer key, repeating the table numbers instead of closing the checks. I don’t know exactly how many minutes because I’m not looking at a clock, and when it’s happening time feels kind of weird, but it’s long enough that service gets backed up and my coworkers have to step in or say my name a few times. Then even after I start moving again, I’m not immediately normal-normal. I’m still trying to figure out what order I’m supposed to do things in.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you notice any involuntary movements—like lip-smacking, chewing motions, or fumbling hand movements—that happen without you meaning to do them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like lip-smacking or chewing, not that anyone’s told me, anyway. What people noticed wasn’t really some small movement like that—it was more that I would just freeze in the middle of service, or start doing things completely out of order.\n\nLike the soda station thing… I was just standing there holding the cash drawer key, and instead of closing checks like I was supposed to, I kept repeating table numbers. Table 12, table 7, table 12—like my brain grabbed the wrong piece of the job and got stuck on it. So my hands weren’t doing the next step, I wasn’t moving on to the register or the checks, I was just sort of stuck there with the key.\n\nSo… I guess if my hands are doing anything weird, I’m not aware of it. The weird part is more that I stop functioning right, or I’m doing the wrong step in the wrong order, and everyone around me can tell something’s off.\n\nDoctor: Right before one of these episodes starts, do you ever notice any warning signs—like a strange smell or taste, a feeling of déjà vu, or a rising sensation in your stomach—just before you freeze up?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing. I’ve tried to think about that, because afterward people ask me, like, “Did you feel it coming?” and I want there to be some clear signal, because then maybe I could catch it before it happens, you know?\n\nBut I don’t remember a strange smell or taste. I don’t get, like, a warning where my stomach rises or anything like that—at least not that I’ve noticed. And déjà vu… I mean, sometimes things feel off or overly intense when I’m stressed, but right before the freezing? I can’t say there’s a specific “oh, this is starting now” feeling.\n\nIt’s more like I’m doing the task, and then suddenly the task doesn’t connect to the next step anymore. Like I’ll be in the middle of work and then I realize I’m stuck, but by the time I realize that, it’s already happening. So if there is a warning sign, I’m not catching it.\n\nDoctor: During these freezing episodes, do you ever notice your body becoming stiff or rigid, or find yourself holding an unusual position for a while, even if someone tries to move your arm or leg?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s stiff like… like someone could lift my arm and it would stay there. I don’t think anyone has tried to move my arms or legs during it, at least not that I remember.\n\nWhat people at work noticed was more that I’d just sort of stop in the middle of service, like my body and brain weren’t going to the next step. The soda station thing is the one everyone keeps bringing up because I was standing there holding the cash drawer key, and I wasn’t closing the checks like I was supposed to. I kept repeating table numbers instead, like, “table 12, table 7,” over and over, and I couldn’t seem to switch back to what I was actually doing.\n\nSo I may be standing still in a weird way, because I’m frozen there, but I don’t know if it’s rigidity exactly. It’s more like I get stuck—standing there, holding something, not moving on—and everyone around me realizes something is wrong before I can really explain it.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of these freezing episodes, have you ever heard voices or sounds that others don't seem to hear, or had beliefs that others found strange or hard to believe?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"Sometimes during closing or when I’m home in the kitchen, I hear a woman’s voice say my name or tell me I’m doing the count wrong, clear enough that I answer before I realize nobody else heard it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, yes. That’s actually something I’ve been kind of embarrassed to say out loud, because it sounds worse when I explain it.

It’s not constant, and it’s not like a whole conversation usually. But sometimes when I’m closing at work, especially doing the count or going over the register stuff, I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name. Or she’ll say something like I’m doing the count wrong. And it’s clear enough that I react to it—like I’ll answer, or I’ll look around, or I’ll say, “What?” before I realize nobody else heard anything.

It’s happened at home too, mostly when I’m in the kitchen. Same kind of thing, like my name, or a comment that feels directed at what I’m doing. And then I feel stupid because there’s nobody there, or if someone is nearby they’ll look at me like, “Who are you talking to?” So I try not to respond now, but sometimes it catches me before I can think.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Right before one of these episodes starts, do you ever notice any warning signs—like a strange smell or taste, a feeling of déjà vu, or a rising sensation in your stomach—just before you freeze up?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing. I’ve tried to think about that, because afterward people ask me, like, “Did you feel it coming?” and I want there to be some clear signal, because then maybe I could catch it before it happens, you know?\n\nBut I don’t remember a strange smell or taste. I don’t get, like, a warning where my stomach rises or anything like that—at least not that I’ve noticed. So if there is a warning sign, I’m not catching it.\n\nDoctor: During these freezing episodes, do you ever notice your body becoming stiff or rigid, or find yourself holding an unusual position for a while, even if someone tries to move your arm or leg?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s stiff like… like someone could lift my arm and it would stay there. I don’t think anyone has tried to move my arms or legs during it, at least not that I remember.\n\nWhat people at work noticed was more that I’d just sort of stop in the middle of service, like my body and brain weren’t going to the next step. The soda station thing is the one everyone keeps bringing up because I was standing there holding the cash drawer key, and I wasn’t closing the checks like I was supposed to. I kept repeating table numbers instead, like, “table 12, table 7,” over and over, and I couldn’t seem to switch back to what I was actually doing.\n\nSo I may be standing still in a weird way, because I’m frozen there, but I don’t know if it’s rigidity exactly. It’s more like I get stuck—standing there, holding something, not moving on—and everyone around me realizes something is wrong before I can really explain it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these freezing episodes, have you ever heard voices or sounds that others don't seem to hear, or had beliefs that others found strange or hard to believe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, yes. That’s actually something I’ve been kind of embarrassed to say out loud, because it sounds worse when I explain it.\n\nIt’s not constant, and it’s not like a whole conversation usually. But sometimes when I’m closing at work, especially doing the count or going over the register stuff, I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name. Or she’ll say something like I’m doing the count wrong. And it’s clear enough that I react to it—like I’ll answer, or I’ll look around, or I’ll say, “What?” before I realize nobody else heard anything.\n\nIt’s happened at home too, mostly when I’m in the kitchen. Same kind of thing, like my name, or a comment that feels directed at what I’m doing. And then I feel stupid because there’s nobody there, or if someone is nearby they’ll look at me like, “Who are you talking to?” So I try not to respond now, but sometimes it catches me before I can think.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever been diagnosed with epilepsy, a seizure disorder, a brain injury, or any other neurological or medical condition?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not that I know of. I’ve never been told I have epilepsy or a seizure disorder or anything like that.

I mean, I’ve had regular doctor visits here and there, but nobody has ever sat me down and said, “This is neurological,” or “You had a seizure,” or anything. And I don’t think I’ve had a brain injury… not anything major that I can think of. Like, I’ve bumped my head before in normal life, but not some big accident or diagnosis.

So… I guess the answer is no, unless there’s something someone missed. But officially, no, I haven’t been diagnosed with any of those.

But sometimes when I’m closing at work, especially doing the count or going over the register stuff, I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name. Or she’ll say something like I’m doing the count wrong. And it’s clear enough that I react to it—like I’ll answer, or I’ll look around, or I’ll say, “What?” before I realize nobody else heard anything.\n\nIt’s happened at home too, mostly when I’m in the kitchen. Same kind of thing, like my name, or a comment that feels directed at what I’m doing. And then I feel stupid because there’s nobody there, or if someone is nearby they’ll look at me like, “Who are you talking to?” So I try not to respond now, but sometimes it catches me before I can think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with epilepsy, a seizure disorder, a brain injury, or any other neurological or medical condition?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I’ve never been told I have epilepsy or a seizure disorder or anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had regular doctor visits here and there, but nobody has ever sat me down and said, “This is neurological,” or “You had a seizure,” or anything. And I don’t think I’ve had a brain injury… not anything major that I can think of. Like, I’ve bumped my head before in normal life, but not some big accident or diagnosis.\n\nSo… I guess the answer is no, unless there’s something someone missed. But officially, no, I haven’t been diagnosed with any of those.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver what period of time—weeks, months, or longer—have you been experiencing both the voice and these freezing/disorganized episodes together?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

I’d say… about four months where it’s really been both together. Like, where the voices were clear enough that I knew it wasn’t just me mishearing something, and around the same stretch of time I started having those freezing or getting-stuck moments at work.

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.   }

Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S022\",\"name\":\"Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\",\"description\":\"A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\",\"reason\":\"The question is asking about abnormal or involuntary behaviors during the same freezing/disorganized episodes at work, which most directly relates to the patient's disorganized or abnormal behavior.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Same kind of thing, like my name, or a comment that feels directed at what I’m doing. Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.
